I. 
Introduction and Qualifications 
1 
Q. 
Please state your name and business address.  
2 
A. 
My name is Theodore Poe, Jr., and my business address is 40 Sylvan 
3 
Road, Waltham, Massachusetts 02451.  
4 
 
5 
Q. 
By whom are you employed and in what capacity? 
6 
A. 
I am employed by National Grid USA Service Company, Inc., a subsidiary 
7 
of National Grid USA (“National Grid”), as the Manager of Gas Load 
8 
Forecasting and Analysis.  I am responsible for supervising the 
9 
development of the gas load forecasts for National Grid’s gas utilities, 
10 
including The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National Grid NY 
11 
(“KEDNY”) and KeySpan Gas East Corporation d/b/a National Grid 
12 
(“KEDLI”) (collectively, the “Companies”). 
13 
 
14 
Q.  
Please describe your educational background and professional 
15 
experience. 
16 
A. 
I graduated from the Massachusetts Institute of Technology in 1978 with a 
17 
Bachelor of Science degree in Geology.  From 1981 to 1989, I worked as a 
18 
Research Associate with Jensen Associates, Inc. of Boston where I was 
19 
responsible for developing a variety of computer forecasting models to 
20 
3

analyze natural gas supply and demand for interstate pipeline and local 
1 
distribution companies.  Upon accepting employment with Boston Gas 
2

3 
natural gas resource requirements of the company’s customers and 
4 
managing its resource-planning process.  In 1998 and 1999, I undertook 
5 
the same responsibility for Essex Gas Company and Colonial Gas 
6

8

III. 
Overview of the Companies’ Retail Gas Deliveries 
8 
Q. 
Please provide an overview of the Companies’ historic gas deliveries 
9 
from 2010 through 2014. 
10 
A. 
For KEDNY, actual retail gas deliveries have ranged from 1.339 billion 
11 
therms to 1.671 billion therms to all customers, excluding non-core (SC 
12 
18-5A Non-Core Transport) and electric generation (SC 18 Small 
13 
Electric Generator Transport and SC 20 Large Electric Generator 
14 
Transport) (Exhibit ___ (TEP-1A)).  Retail refers to gas delivered and 
15 
metered at the customers’ burner tips, while wholesale refers to gas 
16 
received and metered flowing into the Companies’ distribution systems.  
17 
The range in volumes of actual retail gas deliveries was primarily driven 
18 
by fluctuations in the weather such as the abnormally warm winter of 
19 
2011/12 and the prolonged cold experienced in the winters of 2013/14 
20 
9


--- Page 12 ---
Testimony of Theodore Poe, Jr. 
Page 8 of 45 
 
and 2014/15.  On a weather-normalized basis, KEDNY’s retail gas 
1 
deliveries to all customers, excluding non-core and electric generation, 
2 
have grown at a compound annual growth rate of 1.5 percent per annum 
3 
from 1.473 billion therms in calendar year 2010 to 1.564 billion therms 
4 
in calendar year 2014 (Exhibit ___ (TEP-8A)).  This growth reflects 
5 
recovery from the 2007-2009 recession and the decrease in domestic 
6 
natural gas prices.  Historic actual billed sales by month and by class for 
7 
2010-2014 are provided in Exhibit ___ (TEP-7A) for KEDNY.  Historic 
8 
weather-normalized billed sales by month and by class for 2010-2014 are 
9 
provided in Exhibit ___ (TEP-8A) for KEDNY. 
10 
 
11 
For KEDLI, actual retail gas deliveries have ranged from 0.789 billion 
12 
therms to 1.011 billion therms to all customers, excluding small and large 
13 
electric generation (SC 7 Small Electric Generator Transport and SC 14 
14 
Large Electric Generator Transport) (Exhibit ___ (TEP-1B)).  Similar to 
15 
KEDNY, the range in volumes of actual retail gas deliveries for KEDLI 
16 
was primarily driven by weather fluctuations.  On a weather-normalized 
17 
basis, KEDLI’s retail gas deliveries to all customers, excluding non-core 
18 
and electric generation have grown at a compound annual growth rate of 
19 
1.6 percent per annum from 0.892 billion therms in calendar year 2010 to 
20 
0.951 billion therms in calendar year 2014 (Exhibit ___ (TEP-8B)).  
21

IV. 
Gas Delivery Forecast 
4 
Q. 
Please describe KEDNY’s individual service and revenue class 
5 
distinctions. 
6 
A. 
KEDNY’s gas tariff (PSC No. 12) includes the following active service 
7 
classifications: SC1, SC2, SC3, SC4, SC6, SC7, SC17, SC18, SC20, and 
8 
SC21.  Certain service classifications contain distinct revenue classes. 
9 
The revenue classes include Residential Non-Heat, Residential Heat, 
10 
Commercial Non-Heat, Commercial Heat, and Industrial.  KEDNY 
11 
developed forecast models at the rate code level for the revenue classes.  
12 
For example, for the residential heating class, KEDNY developed 
13 
separate models for SC 1B Residential Heat, SC 17-1B Residential Heat 
14 
Monthly Balancing, SC 2-2 Non-Residential Heat, and SC 17-2-2 Non-
15 
Residential Heat Monthly Balancing.  As shown in Exhibit ___ (TEP-
16 
1A), KEDNY has 34 distinct categories within its service classifications 
17 
and each category is modeled using one or more sub-models with the 
18 
exception of the SC 18-5A Non-Core Transport, SC 18 Small Electric 
19 
Generator Transport, and SC 20 Large Electric Generator Transport.  The 
20 
12


--- Page 15 ---
Testimony of Theodore Poe, Jr. 
Page 11 of 45 
 
categories modeled are those for which KEDNY must make gas supply 
1 
capacity planning decisions. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please describe KEDLI’s individual service and revenue class 
4 
distinctions. 
5 
A.  
KEDLI’s gas tariff (PSC No. 1) includes the following active service 
6 
classifications: SC1, SC2, SC3, SC4, SC5, SC7, SC9, SC12, SC13, 
7 
SC14, SC15, and SC16.  Certain service classifications contain distinct 
8 
revenue classes. The revenue classes include Residential Non-Heat, 
9 
Residential Heat, Commercial Non-Heat, Commercial Heat, and 
10 
Industrial.  KEDLI developed forecast models at the rate code level for 
11 
the revenue classes.  For example, for the residential heating class, 
12 
KEDLI developed separate models for SC 1B Residential Heat, SC 5-1B 
13 
Residential Heat Monthly Balancing, SC 2B Non-Residential Heat, and 
14 
SC 5-2B Non-Residential Heat Monthly Balancing.  As shown in Exhibit 
15 
___ (TEP-1B), KEDLI has 39 distinct categories within its service 
16 
classifications and each category is modeled using one or more sub-
17 
models with the exception of the SC 7 Small Electric Generator 
18 
Transport and SC 14 Large Electric Generator Transport.  The categories 
19 
modeled are those for which KEDLI must make gas supply capacity 
20 
planning decisions. 
21 
13


--- Page 16 ---
Testimony of Theodore Poe, Jr. 
Page 12 of 45 
 
Q. 
Please describe how you derived the delivery forecasts for KEDNY 
1 
and KEDLI. 
2 
A. 
On an annual basis, the Companies prepare their gas load forecasts based 
3 
on econometric/statistical forecast models developed for each residential, 
4 
commercial/industrial, and multifamily rate class, with the exception of 
5 
the deliveries to certain rate categories listed above.  The instant forecasts 
6 
were based on historical billing data from March 2003 through February 
7 
2015.  Two different models were developed for each class: (1) a model 
8 
to forecast the number of customers, and (2) a model to forecast use-per-
9 
customer.  In the number of customer (meter count) models, the 
10 
independent variables are a combination of time trends and economic 
11 
variables such as population, households, employment, and gas and oil 
12 
prices.  In the model calculating use-per-customer, the independent 
13 
variables are a combination of time trends, heating degree days, and 
14 
economic variables such as population, housing stock, income, 
15 
employment, unemployment, gross domestic product, and gas and oil 
16 
prices.  KEDNY and KEDLI obtained the historic data and forecasts of 
17 
the independent variables from a leading economic research and 
18 
forecasting firm and energy prices from the U.S. Department of Energy, 
19 
Energy Information Administration, and used this data to develop the 
20 
econometric models and generate load forecasts.  The meter count and 
21 
14


--- Page 17 ---
Testimony of Theodore Poe, Jr. 
Page 13 of 45 
 
use-per-customer models are developed using linear and logistic 
1 
regression techniques and they are selected based on their R squared and 
2 
residual variance after accounting for the effects of autocorrelation and 
3 
heteroscedasticity.  The total energy delivery forecasts are the result of 
4 
customer count forecasts multiplied by use-per-customer forecasts that 
5 
are modeled separately. 
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Please describe how you derived the customer count forecasts. 
8 
A. 
The Companies’ customer count forecast models consist of two 
9 
components.  One component captures the annual changes in customer 
10 
counts.  The other component captures the monthly or seasonal variation 
11 
of customer counts over the course of a year.  The annual changes are 
12 
modeled using linear regression analysis with customer counts as a 
13 
function of economic and demographic variables and statistical trend 
14 
analysis.  The monthly variation is modeled by fitting logistic functions 
15 
that capture the seasonal decline in customer counts during the summer 
16 
months and the subsequent increase during the winter months.  The 
17 
monthly results of these logistic models are then reconciled to the results 
18 
of the annual regression models to establish a monthly pattern over the 
19 
course of the five-year forecast period.  The Companies obtained the 
20 
historic and projected economic and demographic data used as the 
21 
15


--- Page 18 ---
Testimony of Theodore Poe, Jr. 
Page 14 of 45 
 
independent variables in the regression equations from Moody’s 
1 
economy.com (publication date: February 2015).  The customer count 
2 
models for each rate class and the data used to derive them are described 
3 
in more detail below.  Details regarding the meter count modeling are 
4 
provided in Exhibit ___ (TEP-10A) for KEDNY and in Exhibit ___ 
5 
(TEP-10C) for KEDLI. 
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Please describe how you derived the use-per-customer forecasts. 
8 
A. 
The historic monthly use-per-customer values are obtained by dividing 
9 
the total billed therms for each month by the number of billed customers 
10 
for the month.  This data series is then modeled as a function of 
11 
independent variables such as weather, seasonal factors, economic and 
12 
demographic variables, natural gas prices, oil prices and time trends.  The 
13 
specific independent variables used in the models vary by rate class 
14 
depending on the statistical results of the analysis and the goodness-of-fit 
15 
of the equations to the data.  The use-per-customer dependent variable is 
16 
modeled as two components.  The first component captures the annual 
17 
trend in the baseload, or non-heating load, of the use-per-customer for 
18 
each rate class.  The second component captures the trend in the heating 
19 
load of use-per-customer as it relates to heating degree days.  The 
20 
monthly fluctuations in use-per-customer are captured by determining 
21 
16


--- Page 19 ---
Testimony of Theodore Poe, Jr. 
Page 15 of 45 
 
monthly coefficients on degree days and are further adjusted by monthly 
1 
alpha factors, which capture the non-linear relationship between 
2 
consumption and degree days.  The monthly alpha factors are modeled as 
3 
the ratio of the fitted values of the regression equations to the actual 
4 
values to correct for the linear nature of regressions.  The Companies 
5 
obtained the economic and demographic data used as the independent 
6 
variables in these equations from Moody’s economy.com, energy price 
7 
data from the U.S. Department of Energy, Energy Information 
8 
Administration (“DOE/EIA”), and the weather data from the National 
9 
Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (“NOAA”), National Weather 
10 
Service (“NWS”).  The use-per-customer models for each rate class and 
11 
the data used to derive them are described in more detail below.  Details 
12 
regarding the use-per-customer modeling are provided in Exhibit ___ 
13 
(TEP-10B) for KEDNY and in Exhibit ___ (TEP-10D) for KEDLI. 
14 
 
15 
Q. 
How is the weather variable in the models determined? 
16 
A. 
Weather is a fundamental variable in modeling the natural gas 
17 
requirements of the Companies’ customers.  The Companies used gas 
18 
day (10am – 9am) heating degree days (“HDD”) based on temperature 
19 
observations from the NWS Central Park, NYC, NY weather station in 
20 
the models.  For the forecasts, as shown in Exhibit ___ (TEP-11), the 
21 
17


--- Page 20 ---
Testimony of Theodore Poe, Jr. 
Page 16 of 45 
 
Companies defined their normal HDD based on a thirty-year average of 
1 
the period from July 1985 to June 2015 as 4,587 HDD. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please explain how the initial forecasts were adjusted for initiatives 
4 
in the Companies’ service territories that could impact the amount of 
5 
natural gas consumed over the next five years. 
6 
A. 
The Companies’ volume forecasts are based on models of meter count 
7 
and use-per-customer developed for each residential, 
8 
commercial/industrial, and multifamily rate class.  The dependent use-
9 
per-customer variable in the Companies’ load forecast models is the 
10 
quotient of historic sales data divided by historic meter count.  The 
11 
Companies’ historic sales data includes the impact of actual energy 
12 
efficiency savings from both NYSERDA and KEDNY/KEDLI sponsored 
13 
energy efficiency programs.  The forecasts include this trend in 
14 
continuing load reduction based on the historic successes in energy 
15 
efficiency reductions in load.  No further adjustments were made to the 
16 
forecasts to reflect additional energy efficiency reductions. 
17 
 
18 
The Companies also made an adjustment to include the forecast 
19 
migration of 13,403 KEDNY non-heat customers in rate class SC 1A to 
20 
the SC 1B heating class effective July 2016.  KEDLI has included the 
21 
18


--- Page 21 ---
Testimony of Theodore Poe, Jr. 
Page 17 of 45 
 
forecast migration of 6,275 non-heat customers in rate class SC 1A to the 
1 
SC 1B heating class effective July 2016.   
2 
 
3 
Finally, KEDNY’s forecast was adjusted to reflect the rate class 
4 
migration of one large-volume customer from SC 18-5A to SC 17-2-1, 
5 
which is scheduled to become effective January 2016. 
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Has the forecast methodology that you described been used 
8 
previously by the Companies? 
9 
A. 
Yes.  The forecast methodology described here has been used by 
10 
KEDNY and KEDLI for business, gas resource and infrastructure 
11 
planning for a number of years. 
12 
 
13 
Q. 
Please describe the annual forecast deliveries and the year-end 
14 
customer count forecasts for KEDNY. 
15 
A. 
As shown in Exhibit ___ (TEP-4A), KEDNY’s forecast of normalized 
16 
retail gas deliveries is 1.590 billion therms in calendar year 2015, rising 
17 
to 1.689 billion therms in calendar year 2019 (excluding non-core and 
18 
electric generation).  Natural gas deliveries are projected to increase by 
19 
24.750 million therms per year or a compound average annual growth 
20 
rate of 1.5 percent per annum.

Q. 
Please discuss the models used by the Companies to forecast sales 
8 
and customer counts. 
9 
A. 
As I explained previously, the Companies load growth forecasts are 
10 
based on econometric/statistical forecast models developed for each 
11 
residential, commercial/industrial, and multifamily rate class and that two 
12 
different models were developed for each class: (1) a model to forecast 
13 
the number of customers (meter count), and (2) a model to forecast use-
14 
per-customer.  I also noted that in the meter count models, the 
15 
independent variables are a combination of time trends, population, 
16 
households, employment, and gas and oil prices.  In the models used to 
17 
calculate use-per-customer, the independent variables are a combination 
18 
of time trends, heating degree days, population, housing stock, income, 
19 
employment, unemployment, gross domestic product, and gas and oil 
20 
prices.   The details of the statistical modeling are provided in Exhibit 
21 
22

Testimony of Theodore Poe, Jr. 
Page 21 of 45 
 
___ (TEP-10A) (KEDNY meter count), Exhibit ___ (TEP-10B) 
1 
(KEDNY use-per-customer), Exhibit ___ (TEP-10C) (KEDLI meter 
2 
count), and Exhibit ___ (TEP-10D) (KEDLI use-per-customer). 
3

KEDNY Residential Heating 
5 
 
KEDNY Residential Heating Meter Counts 
6 
 
In KEDNY, SC 1B Residential Heating and SC 17-1B Residential 
7 
Heating Transport classes alone accounted for 534,281 customers at year 
8

10

13 
 
14 
Annual customer counts for the Residential Heating classes were 
15 
modeled primarily as functions of time variables.  The monthly variation 
16 
in customer counts was modeled using logistic functions that capture the 
17 
seasonal decline in customer counts in the summer months and the 
18 
subsequent increase in the winter months.  The annual and monthly 
19 
customer count models were then reconciled to produce the forecast of 
20 
monthly customer counts through 2019.

8

9

10 
 
11 
Use-per-customer for the Residential Heating classes was modeled as 
12 
two components.  The first component captures base load, or non-heating 
13 
load, per customer, and the second component captures heating load per 
14 
customer.  Base load use-per-customer models for the Residential 
15 
Heating classes were developed based on annual data as a function of 
16 
economic variables such as employment and time.  The heating load 
17 
component of the model captures the monthly fluctuation of gas demand 
18 
for this class.  KEDNY modeled monthly heating load use-per-customer 
19 
as a function of heating degree days.  To capture the non-linear nature of 
20 
the relationship between monthly use-per-customer and heating degree 
21 
24

days, KEDNY calculated alpha factors that are modeled as the ratio of 
1 
the fitted values of the regression equations to the actual values to correct 
2 
for the linear nature of regressions.

per customer.  Base load use-per-customer models were developed based
